package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    private Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

    @Test
    public void test_addTwoPositiveIntegers() {
        // Verifies basic functionality with standard positive inputs. If a is 2 and b is 3, then the method should return 5.
        assertEquals(5, calculator.add(2, 3));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addTwoNegativeIntegers() {
        // Verifies functionality with negative inputs. If a is -2 and b is -3, then the method should return -5.
        assertEquals(-5, calculator.add(-2, -3));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addPositiveAndNegativeIntegers() {
        // Verifies functionality when adding a positive and a negative integer. If a is 5 and b is -2, then the method should return 3.
        assertEquals(3, calculator.add(5, -2));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addNegativeAndPositiveIntegers() {
        // Verifies functionality when adding a negative and a positive integer. If a is -5 and b is 2, then the method should return -3.
        assertEquals(-3, calculator.add(-5, 2));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndZero() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with zero. If a is 0 and b is 0, then the method should return 0.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.add(0, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addPositiveAndZero() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with a positive number. If a is 5 and b is 0, then the method should return 5.
        assertEquals(5, calculator.add(5, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addNegativeAndZero() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with a negative number. If a is -5 and b is 0, then the method should return -5.
        assertEquals(-5, calculator.add(-5, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndPositive() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with zero as the first operand. If a is 0 and b is 5, then the method should return 5.
        assertEquals(5, calculator.add(0, 5));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndNegative() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with zero as the first operand. If a is 0 and b is -5, then the method should return -5.
        assertEquals(-5, calculator.add(0, -5));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addMaxValueAndMinValue() {
        // Verifies behavior at integer boundaries. If a is Integer.MAX_VALUE and b is Integer.MIN_VALUE, then the method should return -1.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addMinValueAndMaxValue() {
        // Verifies commutativity at integer boundaries. If a is Integer.MIN_VALUE and b is Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return -1.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndMaxValue() {
        // Verifies behavior with maximum integer value. If a is 0 and b is Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.add(0, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndMinValue() {
        // Verifies behavior with minimum integer value. If a is 0 and b is Integer.MIN_VALUE, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.add(0, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addMaxValueCausesOverflowWrapToMinValue() {
        // Verifies overflow behavior. Since no exception is documented, standard Java integer wrapping is expected. If a is Integer.MAX_VALUE and b is 1, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addMaxValueAndMaxValueCausesOverflow() {
        // Verifies overflow behavior with large positive numbers. If a is Integer.MAX_VALUE and b is Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE - 1 (which is -2).
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE - 1, calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addMinValueCausesUnderflowWrapToMaxValue() {
        // Verifies underflow behavior. Since no exception is documented, standard Java integer wrapping is expected. If a is Integer.MIN_VALUE and b is -1, then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addMinValueAndMinValueCausesUnderflow() {
        // Verifies underflow behavior with large negative numbers. If a is Integer.MIN_VALUE and b is Integer.MIN_VALUE, then the method should return 2.
        assertEquals(2, calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addCommutativityWithPositiveNumbers() {
        // Verifies the commutative property of addition. If add(100, 200) returns 300, then add(200, 100) should also return 300.
        assertEquals(calculator.add(100, 200), calculator.add(200, 100));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addCommutativityWithNegativeNumbers() {
        // Verifies the commutative property of addition with negatives. If add(-100, -200) returns -300, then add(-200, -100) should also return -300.
        assertEquals(calculator.add(-100, -200), calculator.add(-200, -100));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addDoesNotThrowExceptionOnOverflow() {
        // Verifies that the method does not throw an ArithmeticException on overflow, adhering to the implication of standard int arithmetic. If a is Integer.MAX_VALUE and b is 1, then the method should complete normally and return Integer.MIN_VALUE instead of throwing an exception.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_addDoesNotThrowExceptionOnUnderflow() {
        // Verifies that the method does not throw an ArithmeticException on underflow, adhering to the implication of standard int arithmetic. If a is Integer.MIN_VALUE and b is -1, then the method should complete normally and return Integer.MAX_VALUE instead of throwing an exception.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1));
    }


}
package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here
public class CalculatorTest {

    private Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

    @Test
    public void test_addTwoPositiveIntegers() {
        // Verifies basic functionality with standard positive inputs. If a is 2 and b is 3, then the method should return 5.
        assertEquals(5, calculator.add(2, 3));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addTwoNegativeIntegers() {
        // Verifies functionality with negative inputs. If a is -2 and b is -3, then the method should return -5.
        assertEquals(-5, calculator.add(-2, -3));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addPositiveAndNegativeIntegers() {
        // Verifies functionality when adding a positive and a negative integer. If a is 5 and b is -2, then the method should return 3.
        assertEquals(3, calculator.add(5, -2));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addNegativeAndPositiveIntegers() {
        // Verifies functionality when adding a negative and a positive integer. If a is -5 and b is 2, then the method should return -3.
        assertEquals(-3, calculator.add(-5, 2));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndZero() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with zero. If a is 0 and b is 0, then the method should return 0.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.add(0, 0));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addPositiveAndZero() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with a positive number. If a is 5 and b is 0, then the method should return 5.
        assertEquals(5, calculator.add(5, 0));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addNegativeAndZero() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with a negative number. If a is -5 and b is 0, then the method should return -5.
        assertEquals(-5, calculator.add(-5, 0));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndPositive() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with zero as the first operand. If a is 0 and b is 5, then the method should return 5.
        assertEquals(5, calculator.add(0, 5));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndNegative() {
        // Verifies the identity element property with zero as the first operand. If a is 0 and b is -5, then the method should return -5.
        assertEquals(-5, calculator.add(0, -5));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addMaxValueAndMinValue() {
        // Verifies behavior at integer boundaries. If a is Integer.MAX_VALUE and b is Integer.MIN_VALUE, then the method should return -1.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addMinValueAndMaxValue() {
        // Verifies commutativity at integer boundaries. If a is Integer.MIN_VALUE and b is Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return -1.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndMaxValue() {
        // Verifies behavior with maximum integer value. If a is 0 and b is Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.add(0, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addZeroAndMinValue() {
        // Verifies behavior with minimum integer value. If a is 0 and b is Integer.MIN_VALUE, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.add(0, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addMaxValueCausesOverflowWrapToMinValue() {
        // Verifies overflow behavior. Since no exception is documented, standard Java integer wrapping is expected. If a is Integer.MAX_VALUE and b is 1, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addMaxValueAndMaxValueCausesOverflow() {
        // Verifies overflow behavior with large positive numbers. If a is Integer.MAX_VALUE and b is Integer.MAX_VALUE, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE - 1 (which is -2).
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE - 1, calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addMinValueCausesUnderflowWrapToMaxValue() {
        // Verifies underflow behavior. Since no exception is documented, standard Java integer wrapping is expected. If a is Integer.MIN_VALUE and b is -1, then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addMinValueAndMinValueCausesUnderflow() {
        // Verifies underflow behavior with large negative numbers. If a is Integer.MIN_VALUE and b is Integer.MIN_VALUE, then the method should return 2.
        assertEquals(2, calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addCommutativityWithPositiveNumbers() {
        // Verifies the commutative property of addition. If add(100, 200) returns 300, then add(200, 100) should also return 300.
        assertEquals(calculator.add(100, 200), calculator.add(200, 100));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addCommutativityWithNegativeNumbers() {
        // Verifies the commutative property of addition with negatives. If add(-100, -200) returns -300, then add(-200, -100) should also return -300.
        assertEquals(calculator.add(-100, -200), calculator.add(-200, -100));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addDoesNotThrowExceptionOnOverflow() {
        // Verifies that the method does not throw an ArithmeticException on overflow, adhering to the implication of standard int arithmetic. If a is Integer.MAX_VALUE and b is 1, then the method should complete normally and return Integer.MIN_VALUE instead of throwing an exception.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_addDoesNotThrowExceptionOnUnderflow() {
        // Verifies that the method does not throw an ArithmeticException on underflow, adhering to the implication of standard int arithmetic. If a is Integer.MIN_VALUE and b is -1, then the method should complete normally and return Integer.MAX_VALUE instead of throwing an exception.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1));
    }

Integer Overflow and Underflow (Wrapping Behavior)\nSince the return type is `int` and the documentation does not specify throwing an exception on overflow, the method must adhere to Java's standard two's complement arithmetic wrapping behavior.\n- **Positive Overflow**: `add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1)` should return `Integer.MIN_VALUE` (wraps around).\n- **Positive Overflow (Large)**: `add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE)` should return `Integer.MIN_VALUE - 1` (i.e., `-2`).\n- **Negative Underflow**: `add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1)` should return `Integer.MAX_VALUE` (wraps around).\n- **Negative Underflow (Large)**: `add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE)` should return `2` (since `-2147483648 + -2147483648` wraps to `2`).\n\n#### 4. Commutativity\nAlthough implied by \"sum,\" it is good practice to verify that the order of operands does not affect the result.\n- **Commutative Property**: `add(a, b)` should equal `add(b, a)` for various pairs `(a, b)`, including boundary and overflow cases.\n\n### Summary of Test Cases\n| Test Case | Input `a` | Input `b` | Expected Output | Notes |\n| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |\n| Positive Sum | 2 | 3 | 5 | Basic correctness |\n| Negative Sum | -2 | -3 | -5 | Basic correctness |\n| Mixed Signs | 5 | -2 | 3 | Basic correctness |\n| Zero Identity | 5 | 0 | 5 | Zero handling |\n| Zero Identity | -5 | 0 | -5 | Zero handling |\n| Zero Sum | 0 | 0 | 0 | Zero handling |\n| Max + Min | `Integer.MAX_VALUE` | `Integer.MIN_VALUE` | -1 | Boundary values |\n| Overflow | `Integer.MAX_VALUE` | 1 | `Integer.MIN_VALUE` | Overflow wrapping |\n| Underflow | `Integer.MIN_VALUE` | -1 | `Integer.MAX_VALUE` | Underflow wrapping |\n| Max + Max | `Integer.MAX_VALUE` | `Integer.MAX_VALUE` | -2 | Overflow wrapping |\n| Min + Min | `Integer.MIN_VALUE` | `Integer.MIN_VALUE` | 2 | Underflow wrapping |\n| Commutativity | 100 | 200 | 300 | Verify `add(200, 100)` also returns 300 |\n| Commutativity | `Integer.MAX_VALUE` | 1 | `Integer.MIN_VALUE` | Verify `add(1, Integer.MAX_VALUE)` also returns `Integer.MIN_VALUE` |\n\n### Important Notes\n- **No Exceptions**: The documentation does not mention throwing any exceptions (e.g., `ArithmeticException` on overflow). package example;

public class Calculator {

    /**
     * Returns the sum of a and b.
     */
    public int add(int a, int b) {
        return a + b;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the difference a - b.
     */

    }

package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    private Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

    @Test
    public void test_standard_positive_subtraction() {
        // If a=10 and b=5 are provided, then the method must return 5. This verifies basic arithmetic correctness for positive integers where a > b.
        assertEquals(5, calculator.subtract(10, 5));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtraction_resulting_in_zero() {
        // If a=5 and b=5 are provided, then the method must return 0. This verifies that subtracting equal operands results in zero.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(5, 5));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtraction_resulting_in_negative() {
        // If a=5 and b=10 are provided, then the method must return -5. This verifies that the method correctly handles cases where the second operand is larger than the first, resulting in a negative difference.
        assertEquals(-5, calculator.subtract(5, 10));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtracting_zero_from_positive() {
        // If a=10 and b=0 are provided, then the method must return 10. This verifies that zero acts as the identity element for subtraction (a - 0 = a).
        assertEquals(10, calculator.subtract(10, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtracting_positive_from_zero() {
        // If a=0 and b=10 are provided, then the method must return -10. This verifies that subtracting a positive number from zero results in the negative of that number.
        assertEquals(-10, calculator.subtract(0, 10));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtracting_zero_from_zero() {
        // If a=0 and b=0 are provided, then the method must return 0. This verifies the edge case of zero operands.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(0, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_both_operands_negative_result_negative() {
        // If a=-5 and b=-3 are provided, then the method must return -2. This verifies correct arithmetic when both operands are negative and |a| > |b|.
        assertEquals(-2, calculator.subtract(-5, -3));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_negative_minus_positive() {
        // If a=-5 and b=3 are provided, then the method must return -8. This verifies that subtracting a positive from a negative increases the magnitude of the negative result.
        assertEquals(-8, calculator.subtract(-5, 3));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_positive_minus_negative() {
        // If a=5 and b=-3 are provided, then the method must return 8. This verifies that subtracting a negative number is equivalent to adding its absolute value.
        assertEquals(8, calculator.subtract(5, -3));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_both_operands_negative_result_positive() {
        // If a=-2 and b=-5 are provided, then the method must return 3. This verifies that subtracting a larger negative number (more to the left on number line) from a smaller negative number results in a positive difference.
        assertEquals(3, calculator.subtract(-2, -5));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_max_integer_minus_zero() {
        // If a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=0 are provided, then the method must return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This verifies the behavior at the upper boundary of the int range.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_max_integer_minus_one() {
        // If a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=1 are provided, then the method must return Integer.MAX_VALUE - 1. This verifies correctness just below the maximum boundary.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE - 1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_zero_minus_min_integer() {
        // If a=0 and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE are provided, then the method must return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This tests the overflow behavior: 0 - (-2147483648) = 2147483648, which overflows to -2147483648 (Integer.MIN_VALUE) in 32-bit two's complement arithmetic.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.subtract(0, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_min_integer_minus_one() {
        // If a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=1 are provided, then the method must return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This tests the underflow behavior: -2147483648 - 1 = -2147483649, which wraps around to 2147483647 (Integer.MAX_VALUE).
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_min_integer_minus_zero() {
        // If a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=0 are provided, then the method must return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This verifies the behavior at the lower boundary of the int range.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_min_integer_minus_min_integer() {
        // If a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE are provided, then the method must return 0. This verifies that subtracting the same large negative value from itself results in zero.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_max_integer_minus_min_integer() {
        // If a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE are provided, then the method must return -1. This tests the overflow behavior: 2147483647 - (-2147483648) = 4294967295, which overflows to -1 in 32-bit two's complement arithmetic.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_large_positive_values() {
        // If a=1000000 and b=100 are provided, then the method must return 999900. This verifies correctness with large values that do not cause overflow.
        assertEquals(999900, calculator.subtract(1000000, 100));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_large_negative_values() {
        // If a=-1000000 and b=-100 are provided, then the method must return -999900. This verifies correctness with large negative values that do not cause overflow.
        assertEquals(-999900, calculator.subtract(-1000000, -100));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_no_exception_on_overflow() {
        // If a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE are provided (which causes overflow), then the method must not throw an ArithmeticException or any other exception. It must return the wrapped result (-1). This verifies that the implementation handles overflow silently as per standard Java int behavior.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void test_commutative_property_negation() {
        // If subtract(a, b) is called, then the result must be equal to the negation of subtract(b, a). For example, subtract(5, 3) = 2 and subtract(3, 5) = -2. This verifies that the order of operands correctly impacts the sign of the result.
        int a = 5;
        int b = 3;
        assertEquals(calculator.subtract(a, b), -calculator.subtract(b, a));
    }


}

    @Test
    public void test_positive_minus_negative() {
        // If a=5 and b=-3 are provided, then the method must return 8. This verifies that subtracting a negative number is equivalent to adding its absolute value.
        assertEquals(8, calculator.subtract(5, -3));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_both_operands_negative_result_positive() {
        // If a=-2 and b=-5 are provided, then the method must return 3. This verifies that subtracting a larger negative number (more to the left on number line) from a smaller negative number results in a positive difference.
        assertEquals(3, calculator.subtract(-2, -5));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_max_integer_minus_zero() {
        // If a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=0 are provided, then the method must return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This verifies the behavior at the upper boundary of the int range.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 0));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_max_integer_minus_one() {
        // If a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=1 are provided, then the method must return Integer.MAX_VALUE - 1. This verifies correctness just below the maximum boundary.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE - 1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_zero_minus_min_integer() {
        // If a=0 and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE are provided, then the method must return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This tests the overflow behavior: 0 - (-2147483648) = 2147483648, which overflows to -2147483648 (Integer.MIN_VALUE) in 32-bit two's complement arithmetic.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.subtract(0, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_min_integer_minus_one() {
        // If a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=1 are provided, then the method must return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This tests the underflow behavior: -2147483648 - 1 = -2147483649, which wraps around to 2147483647 (Integer.MAX_VALUE).
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_min_integer_minus_zero() {
        // If a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=0 are provided, then the method must return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This verifies the behavior at the lower boundary of the int range.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 0));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_min_integer_minus_min_integer() {
        // If a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE are provided, then the method must return 0. This verifies that subtracting the same large negative value from itself results in zero.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_max_integer_minus_min_integer() {
        // If a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE are provided, then the method must return -1. This tests the overflow behavior: 2147483647 - (-2147483648) = 4294967295, which overflows to -1 in 32-bit two's complement arithmetic.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_large_positive_values() {
        // If a=1000000 and b=100 are provided, then the method must return 999900. This verifies correctness with large values that do not cause overflow.
        assertEquals(999900, calculator.subtract(1000000, 100));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_large_negative_values() {
        // If a=-1000000 and b=-100 are provided, then the method must return -999900. This verifies correctness with large negative values that do not cause overflow.
        assertEquals(-999900, calculator.subtract(-1000000, -100));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_no_exception_on_overflow() {
        // If a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE are provided (which causes overflow), then the method must not throw an ArithmeticException or any other exception. It must return the wrapped result (-1). This verifies that the implementation handles overflow silently as per standard Java int behavior.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void test_commutative_property_negation() {
        // If subtract(a, b) is called, then the result must be equal to the negation of subtract(b, a). For example, subtract(5, 3) = 2 and subtract(3, 5) = -2. This verifies that the order of operands correctly impacts the sign of the result.
        int a = 5;
        int b = 3;
        assertEquals(calculator.subtract(a, b), -calculator.subtract(b, a));
    }
}

     9	public class THIS_IS_A_UNIQUE_NAME_Test {
    10	    private Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
    11	
    12	    @Test
    13	    public void test_standard_positive_subtraction() {
    14	        // If a=10 and b=5 are provided, then the method must return 5. This verifies basic arithmetic correctness for positive integers where a > b.
    15	        assertEquals(5, calculator.subtract(10, 5));
    16	    }
    17	
    18	
    19	    @Test
    20	    public void test_subtraction_resulting_in_zero() {
    21	        // If a=5 and b=5 are provided, then the method must return 0. This verifies that subtracting equal operands results in zero.
    22	        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(5, 5));
    23	    }
    24	
    25	
    26	    @Test
    27	    public void test_subtraction_resulting_in_negative() {
    28	        // If a=5 and b=10 are provided, then the method must return -5. This verifies that the method correctly handles cases where the second operand is larger than the first, resulting in a negative difference.
    29	        assertEquals(-5, calculator.subtract(5, 10));
    30	    }
    31	
    32	
    33	    @Test
    34	    public void test_subtracting_zero_from_positive() {
    35	        // If a=10 and b=0 are provided, then the method must return 10. This verifies that zero acts as the identity element for subtraction (a - 0 = a).
    36	        assertEquals(10, calculator.subtract(10, 0));
    37	    }
    38	
    39	
    40	    @Test
    41	    public void test_subtracting_positive_from_zero() {
    42	        // If a=0 and b=10 are provided, then the method must return -10. This verifies that subtracting a positive number from zero results in the negative of that number.
    43	        assertEquals(-10, calculator.subtract(0, 10));
    44	    }
    45	
    46	
    47	    @Test
    48	    public void test_subtracting_zero_from_zero() {
    49	        // If a=0 and b=0 are provided, then the method must return 0. This verifies the edge case of zero operands.
    50	        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(0, 0));
    51	    }
    52	
    53	
    54	    @Test
    55	    public void test_both_operands_negative_result_negative() {
    56	        // If a=-5 and b=-3 are provided, then the method must return -2. This verifies correct arithmetic when both operands are negative and |a| > |b|.
    57	        assertEquals(-2, calculator.subtract(-5, -3));
    58	    }
    59	
    60

    64	        assertEquals(-8, calculator.subtract(-5, 3));
    65	    }
    66	
    67	
    68	    @Test

    71	        assertEquals(8, calculator.subtract(5, -3));
    72	    }
    73	
    74	
    75	    @Test
    76	    public void test_both_operands_negative_result_positive() {
    77	        // If a=-2 and b=-5 are provided, then the method must return 3. This verifies that subtracting a larger negative number (more to the left on number line) from a smaller negative number results in a positive difference.
    78	        assertEquals(3, calculator.subtract(-2, -5));
    79	    }
    80	
    81	
    82	    @Test

    85	        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 0));
    86	    }
    87

    92	        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE - 1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1));
    93	    }
    94

    99	        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.subtract(0, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
   100	    }

   106	        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1));

   127	        assertEquals(-1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));

   148	        assertEquals(-1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
   149	    }

   155	        int a = 5;
   156	        int b = 3;

package example;

public class Calculator {

    /**
     * Returns the sum of a and b.
     */
    public int add(int a, int b) {
        return a + b;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the difference a - b.
     */
    public int subtract(int a, int b) {
        return a - b;
    }

    /**
     */
    public int dummy1() {
        return 0;
    }

    /**
     * dummy method that does nothing and has no code
     */
    public void dummy2() {
    }
}

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertDoesNotThrow;

import java.lang.reflect.Field;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    @Test
    public void testDummy2DoesNotThrowException() {
        // If the method dummy2() is invoked on an instance of the class, then no exception (checked or unchecked) should be thrown. This verifies the documentation statement that the method 'does nothing', implying a safe, non-disruptive execution.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        assertDoesNotThrow(() -> calculator.dummy2(), "dummy2() should not throw any exception");
    }


    @Test
    public void testDummy2CompletesSuccessfully() {
        // If the method dummy2() is invoked, then the method must return control to the caller without hanging, entering an infinite loop, or blocking indefinitely. This ensures the method has no hidden side effects like network calls or blocking I/O, consistent with 'does nothing'.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        assertDoesNotThrow(() -> {
            calculator.dummy2();
        }, "dummy2() should complete without hanging or blocking");
    }


    @Test
    public void testDummy2HasNoVisibleSideEffectsOnState() {
        // If the method dummy2() is invoked on an object with known initial state, then all accessible fields and properties of that object must remain unchanged after the invocation. This tests the 'does nothing' claim by ensuring no internal state mutation occurs.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        
        // Capture the state of all fields before calling dummy2()
        Field[] fields = Calculator.class.getDeclaredFields();
        Object[] beforeState = new Object[fields.length];
        for (int i = 0; i < fields.length; i++) {
            fields[i].setAccessible(true);
            try {
                beforeState[i] = fields[i].get(calculator);
            } catch (IllegalAccessException e) {
                throw new RuntimeException("Failed to access field: " + fields[i].getName(), e);
            }
        }
        
        // Invoke dummy2()
        calculator.dummy2();
        
        // Verify the state of all fields after calling dummy2()
        for (int i = 0; i < fields.length; i++) {
            fields[i].setAccessible(true);
            try {
                Object afterState = fields[i].get(calculator);
                if (beforeState[i] == null) {
                    if (afterState != null) {
                        org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.fail("Field " + fields[i].getName() + " should remain null");
                    }
                } else {
                    if (!beforeState[i].equals(afterState)) {
                        org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.fail("Field " + fields[i].getName() + " should remain unchanged");
                    }
                }
            } catch (IllegalAccessException e) {
                throw new RuntimeException("Failed to access field: " + fields[i].getName(), e);
            }
        }
    }


    @Test
    public void testDummy2DoesNotInterfereWithExternalResources() {
        // If the method dummy2() is invoked, then no external resources (such as files, network connections, database transactions, or system logs) should be modified, created, or deleted. This confirms the 'does nothing' behavior extends beyond in-memory state.

        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        assertDoesNotThrow(() -> calculator.dummy2(), "dummy2() should not interfere with external resources");
    }
